> {"result":{"searchDocs":{"nodes":[{"title":"Connect to your database","href":"https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/connecting-to-postgres","content":"# Connect to your database\n\nSupabase provides multiple methods to connect to your Postgres database, whether you’re working on the frontend, backend, or utilizing serverless functions.\n\n## How to connect to your Postgres databases\n\nHow you connect to your database depends on where you're connecting from:\n\n*   For frontend applications, use the [Data API](#data-apis-and-client-libraries)\n*   For Postgres clients, use a connection string\n    *   **Use the [direct connection string](#direct-connection) for single sessions or Postgres native commands**. For example, database GUIs, client applications like [pg\\_dump](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/app-pgdump.html), [migrations](/docs/guides/deployment/database-migrations), [backup-restore](/docs/guides/platform/migrating-within-supabase/backup-restore), or specifying connections for [replication](/docs/guides/database/postgres/setup-replication-external). The direct endpoint is on IPv6, or on IPv4 if the project has the [IPv4 add-on](/docs/guides/platform/ipv4-address).\n    *   **Use [pooler session mode](#pooler-session-mode)** for application traffic from persistent clients on IPv4-only networks,\n    *   **Use [pooler transaction mode](#pooler-transaction-mode)** for application traffic from temporary clients (for example, serverless or edge functions).\n\nThe table below summarizes each mode, its host and port, IP version support per project tier, and what it's best used for:\n\n| Mode                                            | Host:Port                               | Free | Paid | Paid + IPv4 add-on | Best for                                   |\n| ----------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------- | ---- | ---- | ------------------ | ------------------------------------------ |\n| Direct connection                               | `db.[project-id].supabase.co:5432`      | IPv6 | IPv6 | IPv4               | Migrations, `pg_dump`, long-lived backend  |\n| Shared pooler (Supavisor) - session mode        | `aws-[region].pooler.supabase.com:5432` | IPv4 | IPv4 | IPv4               | Persistent backend on IPv4-only networks   |\n| Shared pooler (Supavisor) - transaction mode    | `aws-[region].pooler.supabase.com:6543` | IPv4 | IPv4 | IPv4               | Serverless and edge functions              |\n| Dedicated pooler (PgBouncer) - transaction mode | `db.[project-id].supabase.co:6543`      | -    | IPv6 | IPv4               | High-performance app traffic on paid tiers |\n\n<Admonition type=\"caution\">\n  The IPv4 add-on is not dual-stack: enabling it swaps the project's IPv6 (AAAA) DNS record for an IPv4 (A) record, so the project endpoint becomes reachable only over IPv4.\n</Admonition>\n\n\n## Quickstarts\n\n<div className=\"grid grid-cols-[repeat(auto-fit,minmax(150px,1fr))] gap-6   not-prose\">\n  <NavData data=\"ormQuickstarts\">\n    {(data) =>\n              data.items?.map((quickstart) => (\n                <Link key={quickstart.url} href={quickstart.url} passHref>\n                  <GlassPanel\n                    key={quickstart.name}\n                    title={quickstart.name}\n                    className=\"[&>div]:p-2 flex justify-center [&_p]:text-foreground-light\"\n                  />\n                </Link>\n              ))\n            }\n  </NavData>\n\n  <NavData data=\"guiQuickstarts\">\n    {(data) =>\n              data.items?.map((quickstart) => (\n                <Link key={quickstart.url} href={quickstart.url} passHref>\n                  <GlassPanel\n                    key={quickstart.name}\n                    title={quickstart.name}\n                    className=\"[&>div]:p-2 flex justify-center [&_p]:text-foreground-light\"\n                  />\n                </Link>\n              ))\n            }\n  </NavData>\n</div>\n\n\n## Data APIs and client libraries\n\nThe Data APIs allow you to interact with your database using REST or GraphQL requests. You can use these APIs to fetch and insert data from the frontend, as long as you have [RLS](/docs/guides/database/postgres/row-level-security) enabled.\n\n*   [REST](/docs/guides/api)\n*   [GraphQL](/docs/guides/graphql/api)\n\nFor convenience, you can also use the [Supabase client libraries](/docs/reference), which wrap the Data APIs with a developer-friendly interface and automatically handle authentication:\n\n*   [JavaScript](/docs/reference/javascript)\n*   [Flutter](/docs/reference/dart)\n*   [Swift](/docs/reference/swift)\n*   [Python](/docs/reference/python)\n*   [C#](/docs/reference/csharp)\n*   [Kotlin](/docs/reference/kotlin)\n\n\n## Direct connection\n\nThe direct connection string connects directly to your Postgres instance. It is ideal for persistent servers, such as virtual machines (VMs) and long-lasting containers. Examples include AWS EC2 machines, Fly.io VMs, and DigitalOcean Droplets.\n\n<Admonition type=\"caution\">\n  Direct connections are on IPv6, or on IPv4 if the project has the [IPv4 add-on](/docs/guides/platform/ipv4-address). If your network is IPv4-only and you don't have the add-on, use [pooler session mode](#pooler-session-mode) instead.\n</Admonition>\n\nThe connection string looks like this:\n\n```\npostgresql://postgres:[YOUR-PASSWORD]@db.abcdefghijklmnopqrst.supabase.co:5432/postgres\n```\n\nGet your project's direct connection string from your project dashboard by clicking [Connect](/dashboard/project/_?showConnect=true).\n\n\n## Poolers\n\nSupabase offers two poolers. The **Shared Pooler** ([Supavisor](https://github.com/supabase/supavisor)) is multi-tenant, available on every project, and IPv4-only. The **Dedicated Pooler** ([PgBouncer](https://www.pgbouncer.org/)) is available on paid plans and co-located with your Postgres instance; like the direct connection, it is on IPv6, or on IPv4 if the project has the [IPv4 add-on](/docs/guides/platform/ipv4-address).\n\n\n### Pooler session mode\n\nThe session mode connection string connects to your Postgres instance via the Shared Pooler (Supavisor). This is only recommended as an alternative to a Direct Connection when connecting from an IPv4-only network.\n\nThe connection string looks like this:\n\n```\npostgres://postgres.apbkobhfnmcqqzqeeqss:[YOUR-PASSWORD]@aws-[REGION].pooler.supabase.com:5432/postgres\n```\n\nGet your project's Session pooler connection string from your project dashboard by clicking [Connect](/dashboard/project/_?showConnect=true\\&method=session).\n\n\n### Pooler transaction mode\n\nThe transaction mode connection string connects to your Postgres instance via the Shared Pooler (Supavisor) in transaction-pooling mode. This is ideal for serverless or edge functions, which require many transient connections.\n\n<Admonition type=\"caution\">\n  Transaction mode does not support [prepared statements](https://postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-prepare.html). To avoid errors, [turn off prepared statements](https://github.com/orgs/supabase/discussions/28239) for your connection library.\n</Admonition>\n\nThe connection string looks like this:\n\n```\npostgres://postgres.apbkobhfnmcqqzqeeqss:[YOUR-PASSWORD]@aws-[REGION].pooler.supabase.com:6543/postgres\n```\n\nGet your project's Transaction pooler connection string from your project dashboard by clicking [Connect](/dashboard/project/_?showConnect=true\\&method=transaction).\n\n\n## Dedicated pooler\n\nFor paying customers, we provision a Dedicated Pooler ([PgBouncer](https://www.pgbouncer.org/)) that's co-located with your Postgres database. The Dedicated Pooler runs in transaction mode only - for session mode, use the [Shared Pooler](#pooler-session-mode). It is reachable over IPv6, or over IPv4 if the project has the [IPv4 add-on](/docs/guides/platform/ipv4-address).\n\nThe connection string looks like this:\n\n```\npostgres://postgres:[YOUR-PASSWORD]@db.abcdefghijklmnopqrst.supabase.co:6543/postgres\n```\n\nThe Dedicated Pooler ensures best performance and latency, while using up more of your project's compute resources. If your network supports IPv6 or you have the IPv4 add-on, we encourage you to use the Dedicated Pooler over the Shared Pooler.\n\nGet your project's Dedicated pooler connection string from your project dashboard by clicking [Connect](/dashboard/project/_?showConnect=true\\&method=transaction).\n\n\n## More about connection pooling\n\nConnection pooling improves database performance by reusing existing connections between queries. This reduces the overhead of establishing connections and improves scalability.\n\nYou can use an application-side pooler or a server-side pooler (Supabase automatically provides one called Supavisor), depending on whether your backend is persistent or serverless.\n\n\n### Application-side poolers\n\nApplication-side poolers are built into connection libraries and API servers, such as Prisma, SQLAlchemy, and PostgREST. They maintain several active connections with Postgres or a server-side pooler, reducing the overhead of establishing connections between queries. When deploying to static architecture, such as long-standing containers or VMs, application-side poolers are satisfactory on their own.\n\n\n### Server-side poolers\n\nPostgres connections are like a WebSocket. Once established, they are preserved until the client (application server) disconnects. A server might only make a single 10 ms query, but needlessly reserve its database connection for seconds or longer.\n\nServer-side poolers, such as Supabase's [Supavisor](https://github.com/supabase/supavisor) in transaction mode, sit between clients and the database and can be thought of as load balancers for Postgres connections.\n\n<Image\n  alt=\"New migration files trigger migrations on the preview instance.\"\n  src={{\n    dark: '/docs/img/guides/database/connecting-to-postgres/how-connection-pooling-works.png',\n    light:\n      '/docs/img/guides/database/connecting-to-postgres/how-connection-pooling-works--light.png',\n  }}\n  width={1851}\n  height={907}\n  caption=\"Connecting to the database directly vs using a Connection Pooler\"\n/>\n\nThey maintain hot connections with the database and intelligently share them with clients only when needed, maximizing the amount of queries a single connection can service. They're best used to manage queries from auto-scaling systems, such as edge and serverless functions.\n\n\n## Connecting with SSL\n\nYou should connect to your database using SSL wherever possible, to prevent snooping and man-in-the-middle attacks.\n\nYou can obtain your connection info and Server root certificate from your application's dashboard:\n\n![Connection Info and Certificate.](/docs/img/database/database-settings-ssl.png)\n\n\n## Resources\n\n*   [Connection management](/docs/guides/database/connection-management)\n*   [Connecting with psql](/docs/guides/database/psql)\n*   [Importing data into Supabase](/docs/guides/database/import-data)\n\n\n## Troubleshooting and Postgres connection string FAQs\n\nBelow are answers to common challenges and queries.\n\n\n### What is a “connection refused” error?\n\nA “Connection refused” error typically means your database isn’t reachable. Ensure your Supabase project is running, confirm your database’s connection string, check firewall settings, and validate network permissions.\n\n\n### What is the “FATAL: Password authentication failed” error?\n\nThis error occurs when your credentials are incorrect. Double-check your username and password from the Supabase dashboard. If the problem persists, reset your database password from the project settings.\n\n\n### How do you connect using IPv4?\n\nYou have two options. The Shared Pooler (Supavisor) is IPv4-only on every project tier - use it in either session or transaction mode. Alternatively, add the [IPv4 add-on](/docs/guides/platform/ipv4-address) to your project, which makes the direct connection and Dedicated Pooler reachable over IPv4 instead of IPv6.\n\n\n### Where is the Postgres connection string in Supabase?\n\nYour connection string is located in the Supabase Dashboard. Click the [Connect](/dashboard/project/_?showConnect=true) button at the top of the page.\n\n\n### Can you use Supavisor and PgBouncer together?\n\nYou can technically use both, but it’s not recommended unless you’re specifically trying to increase the total number of concurrent client connections. In most cases, it is better to choose either PgBouncer or Supavisor for pooled or transaction-based traffic. Direct connections remain the best choice for long-lived sessions, and, if IPv4 is required for those sessions, Supavisor session mode can be used as an alternative. Running both poolers simultaneously increases the risk of hitting your database’s maximum connection limit on smaller compute tiers.\n\n\n### How does the default pool size work?\n\nSupavisor and PgBouncer work independently, but both reference the same pool size setting. For example, If you set the pool size to 30, Supavisor can open up to 30 server side connections to Postgres. These connections are shared between the session mode port (5432) and the transaction mode port (6543). Each mode can use up to 30 connections independently, or split them between both, but the total combined connections across both modes cannot exceed 30. PgBouncer can also open up to 30 connections under the same limit. If both poolers are active and reach their roles/modes limits at the same time, you could have as many as 60 backend connections hitting your database, in addition to any direct connections. You can adjust the pool size in [Database settings](/dashboard/project/_/database/settings) in the dashboard.\n\n\n### What is the difference between client connections and backend connections?\n\nThere are two different limits to understand when working with poolers. The first is client connections, which refers to how many clients can connect to a pooler at the same time. This number is capped by your [compute tier’s “max pooler clients” limit](/docs/guides/platform/compute-and-disk#postgres-replication-slots-wal-senders-and-connections), and it applies independently to Supavisor and PgBouncer. The second is backend connections, which is the number of active connections a pooler opens to Postgres. This number is set by the pool size for that pooler.\n\n```\nTotal backend load on Postgres =\n Direct connections +\n Supavisor backend connections (≤ supavisor_pool_size) +\n PgBouncer backend connections (≤ pgbouncer_pool_size)\n≤ Postgres max connections for your compute instance\n```\n\n\n### What is the max pooler clients limit?\n\nThe “max pooler clients” limit for your compute tier applies separately to Supavisor and PgBouncer. One pooler reaching its client limit does not affect the other. When a pooler reaches this limit, it stops accepting new client connections until existing ones are closed, but the other pooler remains unaffected. You can check your tier’s connection limits in the [compute and disk limits documentation](/docs/guides/platform/compute-and-disk#postgres-replication-slots-wal-senders-and-connections).\n\n\n### Where can you see current connection usage?\n\nYou can track connection usage from the [Observability](/dashboard/project/_/observability/database) section in your project dashboard. There are three key reports:\n\n*   **Database Connections:** shows total active connections by role (this includes direct and pooled connections).\n*   **Dedicated Pooler Client Connections:** shows the number of active client connections to PgBouncer.\n*   **Shared Pooler (Supavisor) Client Connections:** shows the number of active client connections to Supavisor.\n\nKeep in mind that the Roles page is not real-time, it shows the connection count from the last refresh. If you need up-to-the-second data, set up Grafana or run the query against `pg_stat_activity` directly in SQL Editor. We have a few helpful queries for checking connections.\n\n```sql\n-- Count connections by application and user name\nselect\n  count(usename),\n  count(application_name),\n  application_name,\n  usename\nfrom\n  pg_stat_ssl\n  join pg_stat_activity on pg_stat_ssl.pid = pg_stat_activity.pid\ngroup by usename, application_name;\n```\n\n```sql\n-- View all connections\n SELECT\n   pg_stat_activity.pid,\n   ssl AS ssl_connection,\n   datname AS database,\n   usename AS connected_role,\n   application_name,\n   client_addr,\n   query,\n   query_start,\n   state,\n   backend_start\nFROM pg_stat_ssl\nJOIN pg_stat_activity\n ON pg_stat_ssl.pid = pg_stat_activity.pid;\n```\n\n\n### Why are there active connections when the app is idle?\n\nEven if your application isn’t making queries, some Supabase services keep persistent connections to your database. For example, Storage, PostgREST, and our health checker all maintain long-lived connections. You usually see a small baseline of active connections from these services.\n\n\n### Why do connection strings have different ports?\n\nDifferent modes use different ports:\n\n*   Direct connection: `5432` (Postgres on your project instance)\n*   Dedicated pooler, transaction mode: `6543` (PgBouncer on your project instance)\n*   Shared pooler, transaction mode: `6543` (Supavisor, multi-tenant)\n*   Shared pooler, session mode: `5432` (Supavisor, multi-tenant)\n\nThe port helps route the connection to the right pooler/mode.\n\n\n### Does connection pooling affect latency?\n\nBecause the dedicated pooler is hosted on the same machine as your database, it connects with lower latency than the shared pooler, which is hosted on a separate server. Direct connections have no pooler overhead but require IPv6 unless you have the IPv4 add-on.\n\n\n### How to choose the right connection method?\n\n**Direct connection:**\n\n*   Best for: persistent backend services\n*   Use for migrations, pg\\_dump, backup and management tools\n*   Network: reachable over IPv6, or over IPv4 if the project has the [IPv4 add-on](/docs/guides/platform/ipv4-address).\n\n**Shared pooler (Supavisor):**\n\n*   Best for: connections from IPv4 networks (IPv4-only on every tier)\n    *   Supavisor session mode → persistent backend on IPv4 networks\n    *   Supavisor transaction mode → serverless functions or short-lived tasks\n*   Use for application runtime traffic (queries, writes)\n\n**Dedicated pooler (PgBouncer, paid tier):**\n\n*   Best for: high-performance apps that need dedicated resources\n*   Use for application runtime traffic (queries, writes)\n*   Transaction mode only - use the Shared Pooler if you need session mode\n*   Network: reachable over IPv6, or over IPv4 if the project has the [IPv4 add-on](/docs/guides/platform/ipv4-address)\n\nSee the [connection method matrix](#how-to-connect-to-your-postgres-databases) at the top of this page for a quick reference, or follow the decision flow in the diagram below to choose the right option for your environment.\n\n<Image\n  alt=\"Decision tree diagram showing when to connect directly to Postgres or use a connection pooler.\"\n  src={{\n    dark: '/docs/img/guides/database/connecting-to-postgres/connection-decision-tree.svg',\n    light: '/docs/img/guides/database/connecting-to-postgres/connection-decision-tree-light.svg',\n  }}\n  caption=\"Choosing between direct Postgres connections and connection pooling\"\n  width={291}\n  height={150}\n/>\n"},{"title":"Using SQLAlchemy with Supabase","href":"https://supabase.com/docs/guides/troubleshooting/using-sqlalchemy-with-supabase-FUqebT","content":"# Using SQLAlchemy with Supabase\n\n## Deploying to auto-scaling servers:\n\nIf you are deploying to:\n\n*   edge functions\n*   serverless functions\n*   horizontally auto-scaling deployments\n\nIt is recommended that you connect with the pooler in transaction mode (port 6543), which can be found on the dashboard by clicking [Connect](/dashboard/project/_?showConnect=true\\&method=transaction).\n\n```sh\n# Example transaction mode string:\npostgres://[db-user].[project-ref]:[db-password]@aws-0-[aws-region].pooler.supabase.com:6543\n```\n\nWhen using transaction mode, you should use the[ `NullPool` setting:](https://docs.sqlalchemy.org/en/20/core/pooling.html#switching-pool-implementations)\n\n```py\nfrom sqlalchemy.pool import NullPool\n\ncon = sqlalchemy.create_engine(url, client_encoding='utf8', poolclass=NullPool)\n```\n\nWhen relying on Supavisor, it's important to pick an adequate pool size. This guide can walk you through the process:\n\n*   <https://github.com/orgs/supabase/discussions/21566>\n\n## Deploying to stationary servers\n\nFor stationary servers, such as VMs and long-running containers, it is recommended to use your direct connection string, which can be found on the dashboard by clicking [Connect](/dashboard/project/_?showConnect=true).\n\n    # Example DB string:\n    postgresql://postgres:[PASSWORD]@db.[PROJECT REF].supabase.co:5432/postgres\n\nThe connection maps to an IPv6 address, and cannot operate in an IPv4 environment.\n\n### Checking IPv6 support:\n\nThe majority of services are IPv6 compatible. However, there are a few prominent services that only accept IPv4 connections:\n\n*   [Retool](https://retool.com/)\n*   [Vercel](https://vercel.com/)\n*   [GitHub Actions](https://docs.github.com/en/actions)\n*   [Render](https://render.com/)\n\nIf you're still unsure if your network supports IPv6, you can run this cURL command on your deployment server:\n\n```sh\ncurl -6 https://ifconfig.co/ip\n```\n\nIf the command returns an IPv6 address, the network is IPv6 compatible.\n\nIf your deployment environment is not IPv6 compatible, then consider:\n\n*   Using the Supavisor pooler in session (port 5432)\n*   Enabling the [IPv4 Add-On](/dashboard/project/_/settings/addons) if you're on a pro or above plan\n\n### Choosing an internal pool size\n\n**Key Pool Settings:**\n\n*   pool\\_size: This sets the maximum number of permanent connections in the pool. SQLAlchemy will create connections as needed up to this limit.\n    max\\_overflow: Allows creating additional connections beyond pool\\_size for temporary bursts in demand. These temporary connections close after use.\n\n```python\n# Example configurations\nengine = create_engine(\n    \"postgresql+psycopg2://me@localhost/mydb\", pool_size=20, max_overflow=15\n)\n```\n\nAs a rule of thumb, if you're using the Supabase Database REST Client, try to limit the connections used by your deployment to 40% of available connections. Otherwise, you can cautiously increase usage to around 80%. These percentages are flexible and depend on your application's usage and setup. Monitor connection usage to determine the optimal allocation without depriving other servers of necessary connections.\n\n## How to monitor live connections\n\nConnection usage can be monitored with a Supabase Grafana Dashboard. It provides realtime visibility of over 200 database metrics, such as graphs of CPU, EBS, and active direct/pooler connections. It can be extremely useful for monitoring and debugging instances.\n\nYou can check our [GitHub repo](https://github.com/supabase/supabase-grafana) for setup instructions for local deployments or free cloud deployments on [Fly.io](http://fly.io/). For a complete explainer on connection monitoring, you can check out this [guide](https://github.com/orgs/supabase/discussions/27141)\n"},{"title":"Troubleshooting prisma errors","href":"https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/prisma/prisma-troubleshooting","content":"# Troubleshooting prisma errors\n\n\n\nThis guide addresses common Prisma errors that you might encounter while using Supabase.\n\n<Admonition type=\"note\">\n  A full list of errors can be found in [Prisma's official docs](https://www.prisma.io/docs/orm/reference/error-reference).\n</Admonition>\n\n\n## Understanding connection string parameters: \\[#start]\n\nUnlike other libraries, Prisma lets you configure [its settings](https://www.prisma.io/docs/orm/overview/databases/postgresql#arguments) through special options appended to your connection string.\n\nThese options, called \"query parameters,\" can be used to address specific errors.\n\n```md\n# Example of query parameters\n\nconnection_string.../postgres?KEY1=VALUE&KEY2=VALUE&KEY3=VALUE\n```\n\n\n# Errors\n\n{/* supa-mdx-lint-disable-next-line Rule001HeadingCase */}\n\n\n## ... prepared statement already exists\n\nSupavisor in transaction mode (port 6543) does not support [prepared statements](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-prepare.html), which Prisma will try to create in the background.\n\n\n### Solution: \\[#solution-prepared-statement-exists]\n\n*   Add `pgbouncer=true` to the connection string. This turns off prepared statements in Prisma.\n\n```md\n.../postgres?pgbouncer=true\n```\n\n***\n\n\n## Can't reach database server at:\n\nPrisma couldn't establish a connection with Postgres or Supavisor before the timeout\n\n\n### Possible causes: \\[#possible-causes-cant-reach-database-server-at]\n\n*   **Database overload**: The database server is under heavy load, causing Prisma to struggle to connect.\n*   **Malformed connection string**: The connection string used by Prisma is incorrect or incomplete.\n*   **Transient network issues**: Temporary network problems are disrupting the connection.\n\n\n### Solutions: \\[#solution-cant-reach-database-server-at]\n\n*   **Check database health**: Use the [Observability Dashboard](/dashboard/project/_/observability/database) to monitor CPU, memory, and I/O usage. If the database is overloaded, consider increasing your [compute size](/docs/guides/platform/compute-add-ons) or [optimizing your queries](/docs/guides/database/query-optimization).\n*   **Verify connection string**: Double-check the connection string in your Prisma configuration to ensure it matches in your [project connect page](/dashboard/project/_?showConnect=true).\n*   **Increase connection timeout**: Try increasing the `connect_timeout` parameter in your Prisma configuration to give it more time to establish a connection.\n\n```md\n.../postgres?connect_timeout=30\n```\n\n***\n\n\n## Timed out fetching a new connection from the connection pool:\n\nPrisma is unable to allocate connections to pending queries fast enough to meet demand.\n\n\n### Possible causes: \\[#possible-causes-timed-out-fetching-a-new-connection]\n\n*   **Overwhelmed server**: The server hosting Prisma is under heavy load, limiting its ability to manage connections. By default, Prisma will create the default `num_cpus * 2 + 1` worth of connections. A common cause for server strain is increasing the `connection_limit` significantly past the default.\n*   **Insufficient pool size**: The Supavisor pooler does not have enough connections available to quickly satisfy Prisma's requests.\n*   **Slow queries**: Prisma's queries are taking too long to execute, preventing it from releasing connections for reuse.\n\n\n### Solutions: \\[#solution-timed-out-fetching-a-new-connection]\n\n*   **Increase the pool timeout**: Increase the `pool_timeout` parameter in your Prisma configuration to give the pooler more time to allocate connections.\n*   **Reduce the connection limit**: If you've explicitly increased the `connection_limit` parameter in your Prisma configuration, try reducing it to a more reasonable value.\n*   **Increase pool size**: If you are connecting with Supavisor, try increasing the pool size in the [Database Settings](/dashboard/project/_/database/settings).\n*   **Optimize queries**: [Improve the efficiency of your queries](/docs/guides/database/query-optimization) to reduce execution time.\n*   **Increase compute size**: Like the preceding option, this is a strategy to reduce query execution time.\n\n***\n\n\n## Server has closed the connection\n\nAccording to this [GitHub Issue for Prisma](https://github.com/prisma/prisma/discussions/7389), this error may be related to large return values for queries. It may also be caused by significant database strain.\n\n\n### Solutions: \\[#solution-server-has-closed-the-connection]\n\n*   **Limit row return sizes**: Try to limit the total amount of rows returned for particularly large requests.\n*   **Minimize database strain**:Check the Reports Page for database strain. If there is obvious strain, consider [optimizing](/docs/guides/database/query-optimization) or increasing compute size\n\n***\n\n\n## Drift detected: Your database schema is not in sync with your migration history\n\nPrisma relies on migration files to ensure your database aligns with Prisma's model. External schema changes are detected as \"drift\", which Prisma will try to overwrite, potentially causing data loss.\n\n\n### Possible causes: \\[#possible-causes-your-database-schema-is-not-in-sync]\n\n*   **Supabase Managed Schemas**: Supabase may update managed schemas like auth and storage to introduce new features. Granting Prisma access to these schemas can lead to drift during updates.\n*   **External Schema Modifications**: Your team or another tool might have modified the database schema outside of Prisma, causing drift.\n\n\n### Solution: \\[#solution-your-database-schema-is-not-in-sync]\n\n*   **Baselining migrations**: [baselining](https://www.prisma.io/docs/orm/prisma-migrate/workflows/baselining) re-syncs Prisma by capturing the current database schema as the starting point for future migrations.\n\n***\n\n\n## Max client connections reached\n\nPostgres or Supavisor rejected a request for more connections\n\n\n### Possible causes:\\[#possible-causes-max-client-connections-reached]\n\n*   **When working in transaction mode (port 6543):** The error \"Max client connections reached\" occurs when clients try to form more connections with the pooler than it can support.\n*   **When working in session mode (port 5432):** The max amount of clients is restricted to the \"Pool Size\" value in the [Database Settings](/dashboard/project/_/database/settings). If the \"Pool Size\" is set to 15, even if the pooler can handle 200 client connections, it will still be effectively capped at 15 for each unique [\"database-role+database\" combination](https://github.com/orgs/supabase/discussions/21566).\n*   **When working with direct connections**: Postgres is already servicing the max amount of connections\n\n\n### Solutions \\[#solutions-causes-max-client-connections-reached]\n\n*   **Transaction Mode for serverless apps**: If you are using serverless functions (Supabase Edge, Vercel, AWS Lambda), switch to transaction mode (port 6543). It handles more connections than session mode or direct connections.\n*   **Reduce the number of Prisma connections**: A single client-server can establish multiple connections with a pooler. Typically, serverless setups do not need many connections. Starting with fewer, like five or three, or even just one, is often sufficient. In serverless setups, begin with `connection_limit=1`, increasing cautiously if needed to avoid maxing out connections.\n*   **Increase pool size**: If you are connecting with Supavisor, try increasing the pool size in the [Database Settings](/dashboard/project/_/database/settings).\n*   **Disconnect appropriately**: Close Prisma connections when they are no longer needed.\n*   **Decrease query time**: Reduce query complexity or add [strategic indexes](/docs/guides/database/postgres/indexes) to your tables to speed up queries.\n*   **Increase compute size**: Sometimes the best option is to increase your compute size, which also increases your max client size and query execution speed\n\n***\n\n\n## Cross schema references are only allowed when the target schema is listed in the schemas property of your data-source\n\nA Prisma migration is referencing a schema it is not permitted to manage.\n\n\n### Possible causes: \\[#possible-causes-cross-schema-references]\n\n*   A migration references a schema that Prisma is not permitted to manage\n\n\n### Solutions: \\[#solutions-cross-schema-references]\n\n*   Multi-schema support: If the external schema isn't Supabase managed, list the relevant schemas on the `datasource` block in your `schema.prisma` file.\n\n```prisma schema.prisma\ngenerator client {\n  provider = \"prisma-client\"\n  output   = \"../generated/prisma\"\n}\n\ndatasource db {\n  provider  = \"postgresql\"\n  schemas   = [\"public\", \"other_schema\"] //list out relevant schemas\n}\n```\n\n*   Supabase managed schemas: Schemas managed by Supabase, such as `auth` and `storage`, may be changed to support new features. Referencing these schemas directly will cause schema drift in the future. It is best to remove references to these schemas from your migrations.\n\nAn alternative strategy to reference these tables is to duplicate values into Prisma managed table with triggers. Below is an example for duplicating values from `auth.users` into a table called `profiles`.\n\n<details>\n  <summary>Show/Hide Details</summary>\n\n  ```sql table_in_public\n  -- Create the 'profiles' table in the 'public' schema\n  create table public.profiles (\n    id uuid primary key,             -- 'id' is a UUID and the primary key for the table\n    email varchar(256)               -- 'email' is a variable character field with a maximum length of 256 characters\n  );\n  ```\n\n  ```sql trigger_on_insert\n  -- Function to handle the insertion of a new user into the 'profiles' table\n  create function public.handle_new_user()\n  returns trigger\n  language plpgsql\n  security definer set search_path = ''\n  as $$\n  begin\n\n    -- Insert the new user's data into the 'profiles' table\n    insert into public.profiles (id, email)\n    values (new.id, new.email);\n\n    return new;     -- Return the new record\n  end;\n  $$;\n  ```\n\n  ```sql trigger_on_update\n  -- Function to handle the updating of a user's information in the 'profiles' table\n  create function public.update_user()\n  returns trigger\n  language plpgsql\n  security definer set search_path = ''\n  as\n  $$\n  begin\n    -- Update the user's data in the 'profiles' table\n    update public.profiles\n    set email = new.email     -- Update the 'email' field\n    where id = new.id;        -- Match the 'id' field with the new record\n\n    return new;  -- Return the new record\n  end;\n  $$;\n  ```\n\n  ```sql trigger_on_delete\n  -- Function to handle the deletion of a user from the 'profiles' table\n  create function public.delete_user()\n  returns trigger\n  language plpgsql\n  security definer set search_path = ''\n  as\n  $$\n  begin\n    -- Delete the user's data from the 'profiles' table\n    delete from public.profiles\n    where id = old.id;  -- Match the 'id' field with the old record\n\n    return old;  -- Return the old record\n  end;\n  $$;\n  ```\n\n  ```sql triggers_on_auth\n  -- Trigger to run 'handle_new_user' function after a new user is inserted into 'auth.users' table\n  create trigger on_auth_user_created\n    after insert on auth.users\n    for each row execute procedure public.handle_new_user();\n\n  -- Trigger to run 'update_user' function after a user is updated in the 'auth.users' table\n  create trigger on_auth_user_updated\n    after update on auth.users\n    for each row execute procedure public.update_user();\n\n  -- Trigger to run 'delete_user' function after a user is deleted from the 'auth.users' table\n  create trigger on_auth_user_deleted\n    after delete on auth.users\n    for each row execute procedure public.delete_user();\n  ```\n</details>\n"}]}}}
> ````

>

>
> Configure the ASGI application to route HTTP and WebSocket traffic.
>
> ```python
> import os
>
> os.environ.setdefault("DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE", "myproject.settings")
>
> from django.core.asgi import get_asgi_application
>
> # Initialize Django before importing code that may use the app registry.
> django_asgi_application = get_asgi_application()
>
> from channels.routing import ProtocolTypeRouter, URLRouter
> from django.urls import path
>
> from myproject.consumers import EchoConsumer
>
> application = ProtocolTypeRouter(
>     {
>         "http": django_asgi_application,
>         "websocket": URLRouter(
>             [
>                 path("api/ws", EchoConsumer.as_asgi()),
>             ]
>         ),
>     }
> )
> ```

>       </head>

>         <Outlet />

> ### Manage application lifespan
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/frameworks/backend/fastapi
>
> Uses an async context manager to handle startup and shutdown tasks for the FastAPI application.
>
> ```python
> from contextlib import asynccontextmanager
> from fastapi import FastAPI
>
> @asynccontextmanager
> async def lifespan(app: FastAPI):
>     # Startup logic
>     print("Starting up...")
>     await startup_tasks()
>     yield
>     # Shutdown logic
>     await cleanup_tasks()
>
> app = FastAPI(lifespan=lifespan)
> ```
>

> ### FastAPI WebSocket Implementation
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/functions/websockets
>
> Configure dependencies and define a WebSocket endpoint in FastAPI.
>
> ```toml
> [project]
> name = "my-python-websocket"
> version = "0.1.0"
> requires-python = ">=3.12"
> dependencies = [
>     "fastapi>=0.137",
>     "uvicorn[standard]>=0.49",
> ]
> ```
>
> ```python
> import fastapi
>
> app = fastapi.FastAPI()
>
> @app.websocket("/api/ws")
> async def websocket_endpoint(websocket: fastapi.WebSocket):
>     await websocket.accept()
>     try:
>         while True:
>             data = await websocket.receive_text()
>             await websocket.send_text(data)
>     except fastapi.WebSocketDisconnect:
>         pass
> ```

> ### Using RuntimeCache and AsyncRuntimeCache
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/functions/functions-api-reference/vercel-sdk-python
>
> Demonstrates implementing synchronous and asynchronous caching patterns within a FastAPI application.
>
> ```python
> import requests
> import httpx
> from fastapi import FastAPI, Request
> from vercel.functions import RuntimeCache, AsyncRuntimeCache
>
> app = FastAPI()
> cache = RuntimeCache()
> acache = AsyncRuntimeCache()
>
> @app.get("/blog")
> def get_blog(request: Request):
>     key = "blog"
>     value = cache.get(key)
>     if value is not None:
>         return value
>
>     res = requests.get("https://api.vercel.app/blog")
>     origin_value = res.json()
>     cache.set(key, origin_value, {"ttl": 3600, "tags": ["blog"]})
>
>     return origin_value
>
> @app.get("/blog-async")
> async def get_blog_async(request: Request):
>     key = "blog"
>     value = await acache.get(key)
>     if value is not None:
>         return value
>
>     async with httpx.AsyncClient() as client:
>         res = await client.get("https://api.vercel.app/blog")
>         origin_value = res.json()
>     await acache.set(key, origin_value, {"ttl": 3600, "tags": ["blog"]})
>     return origin_value
> ```
>

> ````text
> ### Override Output Directory in vercel.json
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/project-configuration/vercel-json
>
> Use this `vercel.json` configuration to specify a custom build output directory, such as `build`, instead of the default `public` or root directory.
>
> ```json
> {
>   "$schema": "https://openapi.vercel.sh/vercel.json",
>   "outputDirectory": "build"
> }
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Write to absolute paths
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/sandbox/multi-agent
>
> Shows how to write files outside the user's home directory using absolute paths.
>
> ```ts
> await alice.writeFiles([
>   { path: '/tmp/output.txt', content: Buffer.from('data') },
> ]);
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Specify Custom Output Directory for Build Artifacts
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/cli/build
>
> Use the `--output` option to specify a custom directory for build artifacts, overriding the default `.vercel/output`.
>
> ```bash
> vercel build --output ./custom-output
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Configure build outputs in turbo.json
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/monorepos/turborepo
>
> Define the directories that contain build artifacts for various frameworks to ensure Vercel can locate them after a cache hit.
>
> ```json
> {
>   "$schema": "https://turborepo.com/schema.json",
>   "pipeline": {
>     "build": {
>       "dependsOn": ["^build"],
>       "outputs": [
>         // Next.js
>         ".next/**", "!.next/cache/**"
>         // SvelteKit
>         ".svelte-kit/**", ".vercel/**",
>         // Build Output API
>         ".vercel/output/**"
>         // Other frameworks
>         ".nuxt/**", "dist/**" "other-output-directory/**"
>       ]
>     }
>   }
> }
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Building a project locally
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/cli/deploying-from-cli
>
> Generates build outputs in the .vercel/output directory for inspection or prebuilt deployment.
>
> ```bash
> vercel build
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Create a starter monorepo with Turborepo
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/monorepos/remote-caching
>
> Use the create-turbo CLI to quickly set up a new monorepo environment.
>
> ```bash
> npx create-turbo
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Override Vercel Output Directory in TypeScript
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/project-configuration/vercel-ts
>
> Use this snippet in a `vercel.ts` file to override the default output directory for a Vercel deployment, directing it to look for the 'build' directory.
>
> ```typescript
> import type { VercelConfig } from '@vercel/config/v1';
>
> export const config: VercelConfig = {
>   outputDirectory: 'build',
> };
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Declare services in config.json
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/build-output-api/services
>
> Define service build targets and their root directories in the top-level configuration file.
>
> ```json
> {
>   "version": 3,
>   "services": [
>     { "name": "web", "root": "web/" },
>     { "name": "api", "root": "api/", "entrypoint": "main:app" }
>   ]
> }
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Test Remote Caching Functionality
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/monorepos/remote-caching
>
> Run a build to verify that Turborepo is correctly caching artifacts locally and in the cloud.
>
> ```bash
> turbo run build
> ```
>
> ```bash
> turbo run build
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Read and write files in a shared directory
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/sandbox/multi-agent
>
> Demonstrates cross-agent file interaction where files created in the shared directory are accessible to other group members.
>
> ```ts
> // Alice writes to the shared directory
> await alice.runCommand({
>   cmd: 'bash',
>   args: ['-c', 'echo "spec v2" > /shared/devs/spec.txt'],
> });
>
> // Bob reads it
> const spec = await bob.runCommand({
>   cmd: 'cat',
>   args: ['/shared/devs/spec.txt'],
> });
> console.log(await spec.stdout()); // "spec v2\n"
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### user.writeFiles()
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/sandbox/sdk-reference
>
> Writes files as the specific SandboxUser. Relative paths resolve to the user's home directory, and the written files are owned by the user.
>
> ```APIDOC
> ## user.writeFiles()
>
> ### Description
> Writes files as this user. Relative paths resolve to the user's home directory, and the written files are owned by the user.
>
> ### Parameters
> - **files** ({ path: string; content: string | Uint8Array; mode?: number; }[]) - Yes - File descriptors to write. Relative paths resolve to the home directory.
> - **opts.signal** (AbortSignal) - No - Cancel the operation.
>
> ### Response
> - **Returns** (Promise<void>) - Resolves when the files have been written.
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### sandbox.fs.chown()
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/sandbox/sdk-reference
>
> Changes the ownership of a file at the specified path.
>
> ```APIDOC
> ## sandbox.fs.chown(path, uid, gid, options)
>
> ### Description
> Changes the ownership of a file at the specified path.
>
> ### Parameters
> - **path** (string) - Required - The path to the file.
> - **uid** (number) - Required - The user ID.
> - **gid** (number) - Required - The group ID.
> - **options** (object) - Optional - { signal: AbortSignal }
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### sandbox.createGroup()
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/sandbox/sdk-reference
>
> Creates a Linux group with a shared directory for collaborative file access.
>
> ```APIDOC
> ## sandbox.createGroup(groupname, opts)
>
> ### Description
> Creates a Linux group with a shared directory at /shared/<groupname>. Files created inside inherit the group, allowing members to read and write shared files.
>
> ### Parameters
> - **groupname** (string) - Required - Group name. Must match /^[a-z_][a-z0-9_-]*$/ and be at most 32 characters.
> - **opts.signal** (AbortSignal) - Optional - Cancel the operation.
>
> ### Returns
> Promise<{ groupname: string; sharedDir: string }>
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Link a monorepo project using Vercel CLI
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/monorepos
>
> Use this command from the root directory of your monorepo to link multiple Vercel projects simultaneously.
>
> ```bash
> vercel link --repo
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Configure build script in package.json
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/errors/error-list
>
> Define a build script in package.json to perform a static build and output to a public directory. This is required for projects without an api directory or vercel.json configuration using older CLI versions.
>
> ```json
> {
>   "scripts": {
>     "build": "[my-framework] build --output public"
>   }
> }
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### sandbox.fs.access()
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/sandbox/sdk-reference
>
> Checks the accessibility of a file at the specified path.
>
> ```APIDOC
> ## sandbox.fs.access(path, options)
>
> ### Description
> Checks the accessibility of a file at the specified path.
>
> ### Parameters
> - **path** (string) - Required - The path to the file.
> - **options** (object) - Optional - { signal: AbortSignal }
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Basic .vercelignore exclusion patterns
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/deployments/vercel-ignore
>
> Exclude specific files and directories from deployment by listing them one per line in .vercelignore at the project root.
>
> ```bash
> image
> private.html
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Configure Static File Path Overrides
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/build-output-api/configuration
>
> Example JSON configuration to serve a static HTML file without the .html extension.
>
> ```json
>   "overrides": {
>     "blog.html": {
>       "path": "blog"
>     }
>   }
> ```
>
> --------------------------------
>
> ### Set custom microfrontends config file for Turborepo
>
> Source: https://vercel.com/docs/microfrontends/configuration
>
> Define the 'VC_MICROFRONTENDS_CONFIG_FILE_NAME' environment variable outside of the Turbo invocation when running 'turbo dev' to ensure the local proxy uses the correct configuration file.
>
> ```bash
> VC_MICROFRONTENDS_CONFIG_FILE_NAME="microfrontends-dev.json" turbo dev
> ```

>   "body": "## Summary\n\nCompletes the consolidated hackathon implementation on one integration branch:\n\n- preserves and extends the Issue #5 LangGraph/Gemini work;\n- adds root `.env` loading with explicit provider selection;\n- adds Breeth-backed scoped evidence memory with non-fatal fallback;\n- adds four reusable workspace renderer families plus constraint updates;\n- adds supplied-candidate setup, real progress, final results, and the full workspace-evidence API path;\n- materially redesigns the candidate interview UI;\n- packages independent Vercel frontend/FastAPI deployments;\n- documents Windows setup, live smoke tests, Postgres/Supabase pooling, and the presenter flow.\n\n## Verified\n\n- Python 3.12.10\n- real LangGraph import\n- `pytest`: **54 passed**\n- Ruff: pass\n- compileall: pass\n- `npm ci && npm run build`: pass\n- `npm audit`: **0 vulnerabilities**\n- deterministic local E2E: 8 questions, 4+ days, workspace event, SQL persistence, exact feedback, memory isolation\n- local browser E2E: candidate select through final results without refresh, all four renderer families\n- deployed API: public `/health`, unauthenticated `POST /api/interview`, full 8-question/4-day E2E\n- deployed frontend: public candidate selection and interview start calling the deployed API\n\n## Public demo\n\n- Frontend: https://buzzprep-web.vercel.app\n- API: https://buzzprep-api.vercel.app\n- Health: https://buzzprep-api.vercel.app/health\n\nThe deployed profile is deliberately marked as a deterministic demo: `LLM_PROVIDER=fake`, `BREETH_ENABLED=false`, and SQLite under `/tmp`. It is public and functional, but not the durable live-provider submission profile.\n\n## Remaining external gates\n\nThis PR intentionally stays draft and must not be merged as the final release until:\n\n- `LLM_API_KEY` is supplied and `python scripts/smoke_gemini.py` passes against `gemini-3.6-flash`;\n- `BREETH_API_KEY` is supplied and `python scripts/smoke_breeth.py` passes;\n- a persistent Postgres project/connection string is supplied (Supabase transaction pooler is supported with `NullPool` and prepared statements disabled);\n- Vercel production env is switched from fake/`/tmp` SQLite to those live settings and the public E2E is repeated.\n\nRelated: #5 #6 #7 #8 #9 #10 #17"

do not replace it with fake demo state.\n- Keep the visual system extensible for setup/progress/final-feedback work from Issue #8.\n\n## Design direction\n- Modern, deliberate, technical, and interactive rather than generic AI dashboard styling.\n- Avoid excessive rounded cards and huge unused surfaces.\n- Stronger hierarchy and more purposeful use of screen space.\n- The interview should feel like one coherent IDE/simulation experience, not three unrelated panels.\n\n## Acceptance criteria\n- Major visual improvement over the current scaffold on common laptop resolutions.\n- Workspace, interviewer, and candidate context remain immediately understandable.\n- No fake scores/progress/AI outputs introduced.\n- Existing `/api/interview` flow continues to work.\n- Workspace action/state plumbing from Issue #4 remains intact.\n- Layout supports canvas, editor, configuration, logs/metrics, incident and evaluation challenge renderers.\n- Error/loading/empty/disabled states are visually coherent.\n- `npm run build` passes.\n\n## Ordering\nThis is backlog task **Issue 11**, but it should be implemented before the final integration/polish stage rather than left until the end.",

8. **Gemini live:** BLOCKED—no `LLM_API_KEY`; secret-safe smoke script added
9. **Breeth live:** BLOCKED—no `BREETH_API_KEY`; mocked adapter, scoping, retrieval, and graceful fallback pass

11. **Deployment:**
    - [Frontend](https://buzzprep-web.vercel.app)

12. **Production caveat:** currently explicitly configured with `LLM_PROVIDER=fake`, `BREETH_ENABLED=false`, and ephemeral `/tmp` SQLite. It is a functional public demo, not the durable live-provider submission.
13. **Required production variables:** `DATABASE_URL`, `LLM_PROVIDER`, `LLM_API_KEY`, `LLM_MODEL`, `BREETH_API_KEY`, `BREETH_ENABLED`, `CORS_ORIGINS`; frontend requires `VITE_API_BASE_URL`.
14. **Supabase support:** port-6543 transaction pooling automatically uses `NullPool` and disables psycopg prepared statements, following current [Supabase connection](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/connecting-to-postgres) and [SQLAlchemy guidance](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/troubleshooting/using-sqlalchemy-with-supabase-FUqebT).

## Windows demo commands

```powershell
cd D:\Programming\03_Projects\hackathons\AbTalks_hack\BuzzPrep
Copy-Item .env.example .env

```

For a deterministic local demo, set:

```env
LLM_PROVIDER=fake
BREETH_ENABLED=false
```

Backend:

```powershell
cd backend
.\.venv\Scripts\Activate.ps1
python -m uvicorn app.main:app --reload
```

Frontend, in another terminal:

```powershell
cd frontend
npm ci
npm run dev
```

Open:

```powershell
Start-Process http://127.0.0.1:5173
```

Live checks after adding keys:

```powershell
cd backend
.\.venv\Scripts\Activate.ps1
python scripts\smoke_gemini.py
python scripts\smoke_breeth.py
```

## Presenter script

1. Select a supplied candidate and show their curriculum signal.
2. Start the interview and highlight real `1 / 8+` and `1 / 4+` progress.
3. Change a workspace configuration and attach the evidence.
4. Give a strong answer; show the deeper trade-off probe.
5. Give a weak answer; show the targeted diagnostic follow-up.
6. Move through canvas, editor, inspection, and configuration challenges.
7. Complete the hard gate and show evidence-based strengths, gaps, and next steps.

**Next action:** supply the Gemini key, Breeth key, and a Supabase transaction-pooler `DATABASE_URL`, then rerun both smoke scripts and redeploy before merging PR #19.

> # LLM chain: Gemini -> Groq -> OpenRouter
>
> Keep the existing provider-neutral structured generation interface.
>
> Final order:
>
> ```text
> 1. Gemini
>    gemini-3.6-flash
>
> 2. GroqCloud
>    openai/gpt-oss-120b
>
> 3. OpenRouter
>    openrouter/free
> ```
>
> Use current official provider docs.
>
> Groq's `openai/gpt-oss-120b` supports strict JSON Schema structured output. OpenRouter's `openrouter/free` router filters free models according to required capabilities including structured outputs.
>
> Add clean adapters/router, for example:
>
> ```text
> GeminiLLMProvider
> GroqLLMProvider
> OpenRouterLLMProvider
> FallbackLLMProvider
> ```
>
> All must expose the same `generate_structured(...)` behavior and finish with Pydantic validation.
>
> Suggested env:
>
> ```env
> LLM_PROVIDER_CHAIN=gemini,groq,openrouter
>
> LLM_PROVIDER=gemini
> LLM_API_KEY=
> LLM_MODEL=gemini-3.6-flash
>
> GROQ_API_KEY=
> GROQ_MODEL=openai/gpt-oss-120b
>
> OPENROUTER_API_KEY=
> OPENROUTER_MODEL=openrouter/free
> ```
>
> Fail over only for provider availability/runtime failures such as timeout, network errors, bounded 429/5xx exhaustion, provider model unavailable, or invalid provider structured output after bounded retry. Do not fail over for malformed candidate/workspace/client requests or local programming errors.
>
> Internally log which provider served the generation, without secrets and without hidden reasoning.
>
> Add deterministic tests:
>
> ```text
> Gemini success -> no fallback
> Gemini transient fail -> Groq
> Gemini + Groq fail -> OpenRouter
> all fail -> controlled provider error
> fallback result still Pydantic-valid
> 8-question/4-day invariants unchanged
> ```
>
> Run separate live structured smoke tests for all three providers.
>

> # Full modern public LANDING PAGE
>
> Before auth, add a full public landing page. It must feel like a polished product, not a hackathon scaffold.
>
> Core message:
>
> ```text
> Technical interviews should react to what you do.
>
> BuzzPrep turns prep into an adaptive technical workspace.
> Explain. Build. Debug. Adapt.
> ```
>
> Make the differentiator explicit:
>
> ```text
> The interviewer adapts not only to what you say,
> but to what you actually build, configure, connect, inspect and debug.
> ```
>
> ## Design rules
>
> Modern, cinematic, deliberate, technical.
>
> Avoid generic AI-SaaS styling:
>
> - no giant purple/blue gradient blobs;
> - no endless glassmorphism;
> - no every-section-in-a-rounded-card layout;
> - no pill-shaped everything;
> - no huge empty surfaces;
> - no generic four-card feature grids;
> - no robot/brain art;
> - no sparkle-heavy “AI magic” design;
> - no excessive border radius.
>
> Cards are fine where they are the correct information container. Do not make cards the entire layout language.
>
> Prefer:
> - strong editorial typography;
> - low-radius/sharp panels;
> - fine technical borders/dividers;
> - layered real-product workspace visuals;
> - controlled accent color;
> - technical monospace details where useful;
> - compact, purposeful spacing;
> - continuity between marketing and interview workspace.
>
> Keep the existing BuzzPrep identity if present.
>
> ## Landing sections
>
> Build at least:
>
> 1. cinematic hero with strong CTA;
> 2. animated mini interview/workspace sequence;
> 3. scroll narrative:
>    `Scenario -> Act -> Explain -> Evaluate evidence -> Constraint -> Adapt`;
> 4. actual renderer showcase:
>    System Canvas / Editor / Config Lab / Logs-Metrics;
> 5. adaptive interviewer section;
> 6. evidence/memory section;
> 7. curriculum/multi-area coverage section;
> 8. desktop-focused/proctor-ready prep section;
> 9. final CTA;
> 10. polished minimal footer.
>
> Use the real product components/visual language where practical rather than fake screenshots.
>
> ---
>
